**DNS over TLS (RFC 7858).** TCP port 853, TLS handshake, then exactly the DNS-over-TCP framing from §8.4. Cleartext DNS **MUST NOT** use port 853 (RFC 7858 §3.1). Use RFC 7830 padding (EDNS option 12) so that message sizes reveal less.

**DNS over HTTPS (RFC 8484).** Each DNS message is the body of an HTTP exchange with media type `application/dns-message`.

- `POST /dns-query` with the raw message as the body; or
- `GET /dns-query?dns=<base64url(message)>`, where padding characters **MUST NOT** be included (RFC 8484 §6).
- The DNS ID **SHOULD be 0**, so identical queries are identical HTTP requests and can be cached (RFC 8484 §4.1). The response freshness (HTTP `Cache-Control: max-age`) should not exceed the smallest TTL in the response (RFC 8484 §5.1).
- DoH servers **MUST** implement both GET and POST (RFC 8484 §4.1).

**DNS over QUIC (RFC 9250).** UDP port 853. Each query uses its own bidirectional QUIC stream, which avoids head-of-line blocking. The query is framed with the same 2-octet length as TCP (RFC 9250 §4.2). The DNS Message ID **MUST be 0** (RFC 9250 §4.2.1), because the stream already identifies the exchange.

All three carry the unchanged DNS wire format from §4. Your parser and serialiser stay the same; only the I/O layer differs.

## 9. EDNS(0)

### 9.1 Why EDNS exists

The 1987 format has no room for new flags, larger UDP messages or extra RCODE bits. EDNS(0) (RFC 6891) adds them **without changing the message format**. It does so with an extra pseudo-record, **OPT** (type 41), in the additional section. A requester signals EDNS support by including OPT. A responder that supports EDNS **MUST** include an OPT in its response (RFC 6891 §6.1.1). OPT is per-message control data: it **MUST NOT** be cached, forwarded or stored in zone files, and there may be **only one** per message. A query with more than one gets FORMERR (RFC 6891 §6.1.1).

### 9.2 The OPT pseudo-RR: wire layout

OPT uses the normal RR layout (§4.4), but gives the fields new meanings (RFC 6891 §6.1.2, §6.1.3):

| RR field | OPT meaning |
|---|---|
| NAME | Must be the root, `00` |
| TYPE | 41 |
| CLASS | **Requester's UDP payload size**: the largest UDP response it can handle |
| TTL | **Extended RCODE and flags**, laid out below |
| RDLENGTH | Length of all options |
| RDATA | Zero or more `{OPTION-CODE, OPTION-LENGTH, OPTION-DATA}` |

The 32-bit TTL field is split into:

```text
                  +0 (MSB)                            +1 (LSB)
       +---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+
    0: |         EXTENDED-RCODE        |            VERSION            |
       +---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+
    2: | DO|                           Z                               |
       +---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+
```

| Field | Bits | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| EXTENDED-RCODE | 8 | Upper 8 bits of the 12-bit RCODE (§7.2) |
| VERSION | 8 | EDNS version, currently **0** |
| DO | 1 | "DNSSEC OK": the requester wants RRSIG/NSEC records (RFC 3225; RFC 4035 §3.2.1) |
| Z | 15 | Reserved. Send as 0, **ignore on receipt**, and never copy into responses (RFC 6891 §6.1.4; RFC 8906 §3.2.4) |

Each option in RDATA:

```text
                  +0 (MSB)                            +1 (LSB)
       +---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+
    0: |                          OPTION-CODE                          |
       +---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+
    2: |                         OPTION-LENGTH                         |
       +---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+
    4: /                          OPTION-DATA                          /
       +---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+
```

**Unknown options MUST be ignored** (RFC 6891 §6.1.2). Option codes are listed in §19.3.

### 9.3 Worked example: a query with EDNS

`dig +dnssec +bufsize=1232 +nocookie example.com AAAA` sends this (without `+nocookie` it would add a COOKIE option, §9.5.1):

```text
0000  be ef 01 20 00 01 00 00 00 00 00 01 07 65 78 61  ... .........exa
0010  6d 70 6c 65 03 63 6f 6d 00 00 1c 00 01 00 00 29  mple.com.......)
0020  04 d0 00 00 80 00 00 00                          ........
```

*40 octets.*

| Offset | Bytes | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | `be ef` | ID |
| 0x02 | `01 20` | RD=1 and **AD=1** (`0x0020`): "tell me if the data is authentic" (RFC 6840 §5.7) |
| 0x04 | `00 01 00 00 00 00 00 01` | QD=1, AN=0, NS=0, **AR=1** (the OPT) |
| 0x0c | `07 example 03 com 00` | QNAME |
| 0x19 | `00 1c 00 01` | QTYPE 28 (AAAA), QCLASS IN |
| 0x1d | `00` | OPT NAME = root |
| 0x1e | `00 29` | TYPE 41 (OPT) |
| 0x20 | `04 d0` | CLASS = UDP payload size **1232** |
| 0x22 | `00` | EXTENDED-RCODE 0 |
| 0x23 | `00` | VERSION 0 |
| 0x24 | `80 00` | **DO=1**, Z=0 |
| 0x26 | `00 00` | RDLENGTH 0: no options |

40 octets. An OPT with no options is always exactly 11 octets.

### 9.4 Payload size negotiation

- The **requester** advertises the size it can reassemble (RFC 6891 §6.2.3). Use **1232** unless you know the path supports more (DNS Flag Day 2020 recommendation).
- The **responder** sends UDP responses no larger than `min(requested, own maximum)`, treating requests below 512 as 512 (RFC 6891 §6.2.3, §6.2.5). It **must not** exceed the advertised size (RFC 8906 §3.2.5).
- The responder's own OPT advertises the size *it* is willing to receive (RFC 6891 §6.2.4).
- If the response does not fit, truncate (§8.3), and include OPT anyway (RFC 6891 §7).
- Over TCP the payload size is irrelevant. The 65,535-byte limit of the length prefix applies.

The §17 server:

```cpp
size_t limit = 65535;
if (!tcp) limit = q.edns ? std::clamp<size_t>(q.edns->udpSize, 512, AuthoritativeServer::kOurUdpSize) : 512;
```

### 9.5 Options you will meet

#### 9.5.1 DNS Cookies (code 10) — RFC 7873, RFC 9018

These are lightweight proof that the sender can receive at its claimed address, without the full cost of TCP. They defend against off-path spoofing and reflection (§16).

- **Client Cookie:** 8 octets, pseudo-random per client/server pair.
- **Server Cookie:** 8–32 octets. The server computes it from the client cookie, the client IP and a server secret, and returns it.
- Valid OPTION-LENGTHs are therefore **8** (client cookie only) or **16–40** (RFC 7873 §5.2.2). Anything else is malformed and gets FORMERR.
- RFC 9018 defines an interoperable server-cookie format (version, reserved, timestamp, SipHash-2-4 hash), so all servers in an anycast cluster can check each other's cookies.

A client cookie `0123456789abcdef` inside OPT:

```text
0000  00 00 29 04 d0 00 00 80 00 00 0c 00 0a 00 08 01  ..).............
0010  23 45 67 89 ab cd ef                             #Eg....
```

*23 octets.*

`00` root · `00 29` OPT · `04 d0` 1232 · `00 00 80 00` ext-rcode 0, version 0, DO=1 · `00 0c` RDLENGTH 12 · `00 0a` option 10 (COOKIE) · `00 08` length 8 · 8 cookie bytes.

#### 9.5.2 EDNS Client Subnet, ECS (code 8) — RFC 7871

A resolver passes part of the *client's* address to authoritative servers, so CDNs can answer with a nearby location.

| Field | Size | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| FAMILY | 16 | 1 = IPv4, 2 = IPv6 |
| SOURCE PREFIX-LENGTH | 8 | bits of the address that are significant |
| SCOPE PREFIX-LENGTH | 8 | **0 in queries**; in responses, how widely the answer applies |
| ADDRESS | variable | **truncated** to ⌈source/8⌉ octets; bits past the prefix must be zero (RFC 7871 §6) |

`192.0.2.0/24`:

```text
0000  00 08 00 07 00 01 18 00 c0 00 02                 ...........
```

*11 octets.*

`00 08` code · `00 07` length · `00 01` IPv4 · `18` /24 · `00` scope · `c0 00 02` three address octets.

Privacy: resolvers are strongly encouraged to send at most /24 for IPv4 and /56 for IPv6 (RFC 7871 §11.1). Caching ECS answers requires keying the cache by scope (RFC 7871 §7.3). An authoritative server that does not tailor answers simply ignores ECS.

#### 9.5.3 Padding (code 12) — RFC 7830, RFC 8467

Zero-filled bytes that hide the message size on encrypted transports. Recommended policy (RFC 8467 §4.1): clients pad queries to a multiple of **128** octets; servers pad responses to a multiple of **468**. Do not pad over cleartext UDP/TCP, since it gains nothing there.

#### 9.5.4 Extended DNS Errors, EDE (code 15) — RFC 8914

Adds a 16-bit INFO-CODE and optional UTF-8 EXTRA-TEXT to *any* response, explaining the RCODE: why SERVFAIL (DNSSEC Bogus = 6, No Reachable Authority = 22), why the answer was blocked (Blocked = 15, Filtered = 17), whether it was stale (Stale Answer = 3). A message may carry several EDE options. EDE is informational and **must not change how the RCODE is processed**. The text must not be assumed to be null-terminated (RFC 8914 §2, §3).

`INFO-CODE 15 (Blocked)` with text `ad blocked`:

```text
0000  00 0f 00 0c 00 0f 61 64 20 62 6c 6f 63 6b 65 64  ......ad blocked
```

*16 octets.*

`00 0f` option code 15 (EDE) · `00 0c` length 12 = 2 + 10 · `00 0f` INFO-CODE 15 (Blocked) · `ad blocked` (10 octets, no terminator).

#### 9.5.5 Others

| Code | Name | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| 3 | NSID | "Which server instance answered?" A client sends it empty; the server returns an identifier (RFC 5001). Useful behind anycast. |
| 5/6/7 | DAU/DHU/N3U | Client lists the DNSSEC algorithms it understands (RFC 6975) |
| 9 | EXPIRE | Zone expiry for secondaries (RFC 7314) |
| 11 | edns-tcp-keepalive | TCP idle timeout, in units of 100 ms (RFC 7828) |
| 13 | CHAIN | Request a full DNSSEC chain (RFC 7901) |
| 14 | edns-key-tag | Trust anchors a resolver uses, for root KSK rollover telemetry (RFC 8145) |
| 18 | Report-Channel | Where to report DNS errors (RFC 9567) |
| 19 | ZONEVERSION | Zone serial of the answer (RFC 9660) |

### 9.6 Version mismatch and BADVERS

If the query has VERSION > what the responder implements, the responder **MUST** answer with RCODE = BADVERS (16). It puts its own highest VERSION in its OPT, and limits the response to the requester's version level (RFC 6891 §6.1.3). BADVERS needs 12 bits, so it is sent as EXTENDED-RCODE = 1 and header RCODE = 0, because 16 = `1 << 4 | 0`:

```text
0000  22 22 80 00 00 01 00 00 00 00 00 01 03 77 77 77  ""...........www
0010  07 65 78 61 6d 70 6c 65 03 63 6f 6d 00 00 01 00  .example.com....
0020  01 00 00 29 04 d0 01 00 00 00 00 00              ...)........
```

*44 octets.*

`80 00` QR=1, RCODE 0 · AR=1 · question · `00 00 29 04 d0` OPT root/type/1232 · **`01`** EXTENDED-RCODE 1 → (1 << 4) | 0 = 16 = BADVERS · `00` VERSION 0 · `00 00` flags · `00 00` RDLENGTH.

Server-side EDNS handling, as minimal and complete as it can be (RFC 8906 §7):

1. No OPT in the query → no OPT in the response; 512-byte UDP limit.
2. OPT present, VERSION ≠ 0 → BADVERS, empty answer, OPT with version 0.
3. More than one OPT, or OPT not owned by the root → FORMERR.
4. Otherwise respond with an OPT. Ignore unknown options and unknown flags, and never echo unknown flags. Copy DO if you serve DNSSEC records (RFC 8906 §3.2.6).

The OPT parser from §17.3:

```cpp
inline Edns parseOpt(uint16_t rclass, uint32_t ttl, std::span<const uint8_t> rdata) {
    Edns e;
    e.udpSize = rclass;
    e.extendedRcode = uint8_t(ttl >> 24);
    e.version = uint8_t(ttl >> 16);
    e.dnssecOk = ttl & 0x8000;
    WireReader r(rdata);
    while (r.remaining() > 0) {        // each option: code, length, data
        EdnsOption o;
        o.code = r.u16();
        uint16_t len = r.u16();
        auto d = r.bytes(len);         // throws if the option overruns RDLENGTH
        o.data.assign(d.begin(), d.end());
        e.options.push_back(std::move(o));
    }
    return e;
}
```

## 10. Zones and Zone Files

### 10.1 What a zone file is

A **master file**, usually called a zone file, is the text form of a zone (RFC 1035 §5). An authoritative server loads it at startup or on reload. Secondaries receive the same data by zone transfer (§14) instead. The format is also what `dig` prints, so you will read it constantly.

### 10.2 Syntax

The file is a sequence of **entries**. Entries are line-oriented, but parentheses continue an entry across lines (RFC 1035 §5.1).

```text
<blank>[<comment>]
$ORIGIN <domain-name> [<comment>]
$INCLUDE <file-name> [<domain-name>] [<comment>]
$TTL <ttl> [<comment>]                       ; RFC 2308 §4
<domain-name> <rr> [<comment>]
<blank> <rr> [<comment>]                     ; owner = the previous owner

<rr> is either   [<TTL>] [<class>] <type> <RDATA>
            or   [<class>] [<TTL>] <type> <RDATA>
```

| Element | Rule |
|---|---|
| Separators | Any mix of spaces and tabs |
| `;` | Comment to end of line (except inside quotes) |
| `( … )` | Line breaks inside parentheses are ignored |
| `"…"` | A quoted `<character-string>`. It may contain spaces; `\"` is a literal quote |
| Leading blank | An entry that starts with whitespace reuses the **previous owner** |
| Owner | Absolute if it ends with `.`, otherwise relative to the current origin; `@` = origin |
| TTL | Decimal seconds. BIND also accepts units: `1h30m`, `2d`, `1w` (not in the RFC, but universal) |
| Class | `IN`, `CH`, `HS`. Type and class mnemonics never overlap, so the parse is unambiguous. |
| Escapes | `\X` and `\DDD`, in names and strings (§3.5) |

### 10.3 Directives

| Directive | Meaning |
|---|---|
| `$ORIGIN name` | Sets the origin appended to relative names. It can appear several times. |
| `$INCLUDE file [origin]` | Inserts another file. The optional origin applies **inside** the included file, and the parent's origin is **never changed** by it (RFC 1035 §5.1). Restrict which paths may be included if zone files come from untrusted users. |
| `$TTL ttl` | Default TTL for records without one (RFC 2308 §4) |
| `$GENERATE` | BIND extension that creates a series of records, e.g. `$GENERATE 1-254 $ PTR host-$.example.com.` |

**Default TTL.** The original rule was "omitted TTL = the last explicitly stated TTL" (RFC 1035 §5.1). With `$TTL`, the `$TTL` value is used instead (RFC 2308 §4). The §17 parser applies: explicit TTL → `$TTL` → last explicit TTL → a built-in default.

**Unknown types** use the RFC 3597 §5 generic syntax, `TYPE<n>` for the type and `\# <length> <hex>` for the RDATA:

```text
private.example.com.  3600  IN  TYPE65280  \# 4 0a000001
```

### 10.4 A complete example

This is the zone used by the test server in §17. Every construct above appears in it:

```text
$ORIGIN example.com.
$TTL 3600
; zone apex
@       IN  SOA ns1 hostmaster (
                2026102001 ; serial
                2h         ; refresh
                1h         ; retry
                2w         ; expire
                5m )       ; minimum / negative TTL
        IN  NS  ns1
        IN  NS  ns2.example.net.
        IN  MX  10 mail
        IN  TXT "v=spf1 mx -all"
ns1     IN  A     192.0.2.53
mail    IN  A     192.0.2.25
www     IN  A     192.0.2.10
        IN  AAAA  2001:db8::10
web     300 IN CNAME www
*.wild  IN  A     192.0.2.99
a.b.c   IN  TXT   "deep" "name"
_sip._udp IN SRV  10 60 5060 sip
sip     IN  A     192.0.2.60
; delegation of sub.example.com with in-bailiwick glue
sub     IN  NS    ns.sub
ns.sub  IN  A     192.0.2.200
legacy  IN  DNAME example.net.
caa     IN  CAA   0 issue "letsencrypt.org"
```

- `@ IN SOA ns1 hostmaster (` … `)`: the owner is the origin. `ns1` and `hostmaster` are relative names, so they become `ns1.example.com.` and `hostmaster.example.com.`.
- The lines starting with whitespace (`IN NS ns1`, …) belong to `@`. `IN AAAA` belongs to `www`.
- `web 300 IN CNAME www` has an explicit TTL of 300. The others use `$TTL 3600`.
- `a.b.c` creates the empty non-terminals `b.c.example.com.` and `c.example.com.`.
- `sub IN NS ns.sub` is a **delegation**; `ns.sub IN A` is its **glue**. That A record is below the cut, so it is not authoritative data (§10.6).

### 10.5 SOA fields and serial number arithmetic

| Field | Used by | Typical value |
|---|---|---|
| MNAME | NOTIFY targets, UPDATE forwarding: "the primary" | `ns1.example.com.` |
| RNAME | Humans: the contact mailbox | `hostmaster.example.com.` |
| SERIAL | Secondaries: has the zone changed? | `YYYYMMDDnn`, e.g. 2026102001 |
| REFRESH | Secondary polls the primary every REFRESH seconds | 1–24 h |
| RETRY | …and every RETRY seconds after a failure | less than REFRESH |
| EXPIRE | After EXPIRE seconds without contact, a secondary **stops answering** for the zone (SERVFAIL) | 1–4 weeks |
| MINIMUM | Negative-caching TTL (RFC 2308 §4) | 5 min – 1 h |

**SERIAL is compared with serial number arithmetic** (RFC 1982), so it can wrap around 2³² safely. `s1 < s2` iff the forward distance from s1 to s2 is less than 2³¹:

```cpp
// RFC 1982 §3.2 serial number arithmetic for SERIAL_BITS = 32.
// Returns true if s1 is "less than" s2. (s1 and s2 exactly 2^31 apart is undefined.)
constexpr bool serialLess(uint32_t s1, uint32_t s2) {
    return (s1 < s2 && s2 - s1 < 0x80000000u) || (s1 > s2 && s1 - s2 > 0x80000000u);
}
static_assert(serialLess(0xFFFFFFFFu, 0));   // 0 comes "after" 4294967295
```

Consequences: a single change may increase the serial by at most 2³¹−1 (RFC 1982 §3.1). To "reset" an accidentally large serial, step it forward in two increments of less than 2³¹ each, waiting for all secondaries to catch up in between. A secondary transfers the zone only if `serialLess(its_serial, primary_serial)`.

### 10.6 Glue and what lies outside authority

- A delegation to **in-domain** name servers (`sub NS ns.sub.example.com.`) needs glue: their A/AAAA records must be in the parent zone (RFC 1035 §5.2 item 3), and the parent must include them in referrals (RFC 9471 §3.1).
- Names below a cut other than glue are **occluded**. The parent never serves them as answers, because the child is authoritative.
- Records below a DNAME are likewise not allowed (RFC 6672 §2.4).
- Glue is **not authoritative**. A server must never return it with AA=1 as an answer. The §11 lookup reaches it only through the referral and additional-section logic.

### 10.7 Load-time validation

RFC 1035 §5.2 says to **reject the whole zone** on any error. One broken delegation would otherwise make the server give authoritative NXDOMAIN for a whole subtree.

| Check | Reference | Done in §17 `Zone`/`parseZone` |
|---|---|---|
| Syntax errors, unknown types without `\#` | RFC 1035 §5.2; RFC 8906 §7 | yes |
| Every record inside the origin | RFC 1035 §5.2 item 4 | yes |
| Exactly one SOA at the apex | RFC 1035 §5.2 item 2 | yes |
| At least one NS at the apex | RFC 1034 §4.2.1 | yes |
| CNAME with other data, or two CNAMEs | RFC 2181 §10.1 | yes |
| Duplicate RRs collapse to one | RFC 2181 §5 | yes |
| Differing TTLs in one RRset | RFC 2181 §5.2 says treat as an error. The §17 code uses the minimum instead, which is what many servers do. | lenient |
| Single class per zone | RFC 1035 §5.2 item 1 | not checked |
| Required glue present | RFC 1035 §5.2 item 3 | not checked |
| Data below DNAME / cuts | RFC 6672 §2.4 | served as occluded, not rejected |

### 10.8 The tokenizer

Parsing a zone file has two stages. First, split it into **logical lines of tokens**. Then interpret each line. `tokenizeZone()` (§17.5) scans one character at a time:

1. `\n` outside parentheses ends the current logical line. Inside parentheses it is ignored.
2. Whitespace at the very start of a physical line sets the line's `blankOwner` flag, unless that line continues inside parentheses.
3. `;` skips to the end of the physical line.
4. `(` and `)` adjust a nesting counter. An unbalanced `)`, or an unclosed `(` at end of file, is an error.
5. `"` starts a quoted token, which runs to the next unescaped `"` and may contain spaces, `;` and newlines. The token is marked `quoted`, so `"$TTL"` is never mistaken for a directive.
6. Anything else is an unquoted token, which runs to the next delimiter.
7. Backslash escapes are **kept** in the token text. They are decoded later, by `DnsName::fromText` for names and by `appendCharString` for strings, because the meaning of `\.` depends on context.

The interpreter (`parseZone`) then handles each line. It takes the owner, or the previous owner if `blankOwner` is set. Next come up to two TTL/class tokens in either order, the type mnemonic, and the RDATA tokens. The RDATA is converted to wire format by a per-type switch (`parseRdataText`).

## 11. Authoritative Server Logic

### 11.1 The standard algorithm (RFC 1034 §4.3.2)

RFC 1034 §4.3.2 gives the algorithm every name server follows. Condensed:

1. Set or clear **RA** depending on whether you offer recursion. If recursion is offered and RD=1, resolve recursively (step 5); otherwise go to step 2.
2. Find the **zone that is the nearest ancestor of QNAME**. If there is none, go to step 4. For an authoritative-only server, that means **REFUSED**.
3. **Match down, label by label**, from the zone apex toward QNAME:
   - **a.** The whole QNAME matched: you found the node. If it has a CNAME and QTYPE ≠ CNAME, copy the CNAME into the answer, set QNAME to its target, and **go back to step 1**. Otherwise copy the RRs matching QTYPE into the answer and go to step 6.
   - **b.** You reached a node with **NS records** (a zone cut) before QNAME was fully matched: that is a **referral**. Put the NS RRset in the authority section and the available addresses (glue) in the additional section. Go to step 4.
   - **c.** At some label no match is possible: the name does not exist. Look for a `*` label at that level (wildcard). If it exists, match its RRs against QTYPE and copy them with **owner = QNAME**, then go to step 6. RFC 4592 §3.3.3 adds that a CNAME at the wildcard is followed like in 3a. If there is no wildcard: if the name is the original QNAME, set **NXDOMAIN** and exit, otherwise just exit. RFC 6604 has since changed this: the RCODE now reflects the last name in a chain (§11.7).
4. (Resolvers and caching servers) Match down in the cache.
5. (Resolvers) Use the resolver algorithm (§12).
6. Add **additional-section** records from local data, e.g. addresses for NS/MX targets. Exit.

Later RFCs that refine it: RFC 2308 (negative answers), RFC 4592 (wildcards), RFC 6672 (DNAME), RFC 8020 (NXDOMAIN means "nothing below"), RFC 6604 (RCODE and AA in chains), RFC 8482 (ANY), RFC 9471 (glue).

### 11.2 Data structures

You need, per zone:

| Operation | Needed for |
|---|---|
| exact lookup of a name (case-insensitive) | step 3a |
| "does this name exist?", including **empty non-terminals** | 3c, NXDOMAIN vs NODATA |
| walk QNAME's ancestors from the apex down | 3b (cuts), DNAME |
| closest existing ancestor | 3c (wildcard), NSEC/NSEC3 proofs |
| predecessor in **canonical order** | NSEC proofs (§15.6); only if you sign or serve DNSSEC |

The §17 `Zone` uses one hash map from the lower-cased wire name to a `Node` (a map of type → RRset). When a record is added, all of its ancestors up to the apex are inserted as empty nodes, so ENTs exist without any extra bookkeeping:

```cpp
// Make every ancestor between the owner and the apex exist, so empty non-terminals
// answer NODATA instead of NXDOMAIN (RFC 8020).
for (DnsName n = rr.name.parent(); n.labelCount() > origin_.labelCount(); n = n.parent())
    nodes_.try_emplace(n.key()).first->second.name = n;
```

A hash map cannot answer "predecessor in canonical order". DNSSEC servers use an ordered tree keyed by the canonical form (§15.5), such as a red-black tree, a radix tree or a sorted vector.

### 11.3 The complete lookup in pseudocode

```text
function answer(query):
    if query.opcode != QUERY            → NOTIMP                       (§7.1)
    if query has OPT with version != 0  → BADVERS                      (§9.6)
    if QDCOUNT != 1                     → FORMERR                      (RFC 9619)
    zone := longest zone origin that QNAME is equal to or below
    if none                             → REFUSED
    AA := 1
    lookup(zone, QNAME, QTYPE)

function lookup(zone, qname, qtype):
    repeat at most 16 times:                         ; CNAME/DNAME chain limit
        ; step 3b: walk down from the apex, looking for cuts and DNAMEs
        for each ancestor A of qname, from (apex + 1 label) down to qname itself:
            node := zone.find(A)
            if node is null: break                   ; nothing below, handled by 3c
            if node has NS and not (A == qname and qtype == DS):
                return REFERRAL(A, node.NS)          ; §11.8
            if node has DNAME and A != qname:
                append DNAME RRset to answer
                target := qname with suffix A replaced by DNAME target
                if target too long: RCODE := YXDOMAIN; return
                append synthesized CNAME qname → target (TTL of DNAME)
                if target not in zone: return        ; resolver continues from here
                qname := target; continue repeat
        ; step 3a / 3c
        node := zone.find(qname)
        if node is null:
            ce := closest existing ancestor of qname           ; at least the apex
            node := zone.find("*." + ce)
            if node is null:
                RCODE := NXDOMAIN; add SOA to authority; return
        ; node is the exact match or the wildcard: owner of everything copied is qname
        if qtype == ANY: copy one RRset (RFC 8482); return
        if node has qtype:  copy RRset with owner qname; add additional data; return
        if node has CNAME:  copy CNAME with owner qname
                            if target not in zone: return
                            qname := target; continue repeat
        NODATA: add SOA to authority; return
```

The C++ is in §17.7 (`AuthoritativeServer::lookup`). The order matters: **the cut check comes before the exact match**. A name at or below a cut belongs to the child zone even if the parent's file has records there (occluded data, §10.6).

### 11.4 DNAME processing (RFC 6672 §2.2–2.3)

When the downward walk meets a DNAME at a *proper* ancestor `A` of QNAME:

1. Copy the DNAME RRset into the answer.
2. Synthesise `QNAME CNAME <QNAME minus A, plus target>`, with the TTL of the DNAME, and copy it into the answer. Old resolvers that do not understand DNAME can then follow the CNAME.
3. If the new name is longer than 255 octets, the RCODE is **YXDOMAIN** (RFC 6672 §2.2).
4. Continue the lookup at the new name if it is in the zone. Otherwise stop; the resolver will follow it.

A DNAME at QNAME itself redirects nothing: `legacy.example.com DNAME` answers a query *for* `legacy.example.com` normally (RFC 6672 §2.3).

Real output of the §17 server for `x.legacy.example.com A`:

```text
0000  0d 0d 84 00 00 01 00 02 00 00 00 00 01 78 06 6c  .............x.l
0010  65 67 61 63 79 07 65 78 61 6d 70 6c 65 03 63 6f  egacy.example.co
0020  6d 00 00 01 00 01 c0 0e 00 27 00 01 00 00 0e 10  m........'......
0030  00 0d 07 65 78 61 6d 70 6c 65 03 6e 65 74 00 c0  ...example.net..
0040  0c 00 05 00 01 00 00 0e 10 00 0f 01 78 07 65 78  ............x.ex
0050  61 6d 70 6c 65 03 6e 65 74 00                    ample.net.
```

*90 octets.*

| Offset | Bytes | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 0x02 | `84 00` | QR=1, **AA=1**, RCODE 0 |
| 0x06 | `00 02` | ANCOUNT 2 |
| 0x0c | `01 78 06 legacy 07 example 03 com 00` | QNAME `x.legacy.example.com.` (`legacy.example.com` starts at 0x0e) |
| 0x26 | `c0 0e` `00 27` `00 01` `00 00 0e 10` `00 0d` | owner → 0x0e `legacy.example.com.`, type 39 DNAME, IN, TTL 3600, RDLENGTH 13 |
| 0x32 | `07 example 03 net 00` | DNAME target `example.net.`, uncompressed as RFC 6672 §2.5 requires |
| 0x3f | `c0 0c` `00 05` `00 01` `00 00 0e 10` `00 0f` | owner → QNAME, type CNAME, IN, TTL 3600 (taken from the DNAME), RDLENGTH 15 |
| 0x4b | `01 78 07 example 03 net 00` | synthesised target `x.example.net.` |

### 11.5 Wildcards (RFC 4592)

- A wildcard is an owner whose **leftmost label is exactly `*`**. `the*.example` and `**.example` are ordinary names (RFC 4592 §2.1.2).
- A wildcard is used **only if QNAME does not exist**. An existing name, including an **empty non-terminal**, is never wildcard-matched (RFC 4592 §2.2).
- The **closest encloser** is the longest existing ancestor of QNAME. The **source of synthesis** is `*.<closest encloser>`, and only that one wildcard is considered (RFC 4592 §3.3.1). It matches any number of missing labels: `*.wild.example.com` answers `a.b.c.wild.example.com` as long as `c.wild.example.com` does not exist.
- Synthesised RRs get **owner = QNAME** (RFC 1034 §4.3.2 step 3c).
- If the wildcard exists but lacks QTYPE, the answer is **NODATA**, not NXDOMAIN, because the wildcard "makes the name exist".
- A wildcard CNAME is followed like a normal CNAME (RFC 4592 §3.3.3).
- In a *query*, or inside RDATA, `*` has no special meaning (RFC 4592 §2.3). A query for `*.wild.example.com` matches the wildcard node itself exactly.
- Wildcard NS has no defined semantics and is discouraged (RFC 4592 §4.2). Wildcard DNAME is "to be avoided or outright rejected" (RFC 4592 §4.4).

Real output for `x.wild.example.com A`, synthesised from `*.wild.example.com A 192.0.2.99`:

```text
0000  01 01 84 00 00 01 00 01 00 00 00 00 01 78 04 77  .............x.w
0010  69 6c 64 07 65 78 61 6d 70 6c 65 03 63 6f 6d 00  ild.example.com.
0020  00 01 00 01 c0 0c 00 01 00 01 00 00 0e 10 00 04  ................
0030  c0 00 02 63                                      ...c
```

*52 octets.*

The answer's owner at 0x24 is `c0 0c`, pointing to the QNAME. The client cannot tell that a wildcard was involved (with DNSSEC it can: §15.6).

### 11.6 NXDOMAIN vs NODATA, and empty non-terminals

| Situation | RCODE | Answer | Authority |
|---|---|---|---|
| Name and type exist | NOERROR | RRset | (empty, or NS of the zone if you choose to add it) |
| **NODATA:** name exists (with data, or as an ENT), type does not | **NOERROR** | empty | SOA of the zone |
| **NXDOMAIN:** name does not exist and no wildcard applies | **NXDOMAIN** | empty (or the CNAME chain that led here) | SOA of the zone |

- **The SOA in the authority section is required**, because resolvers take the negative-caching TTL from it (RFC 2308 §3). Set its TTL to `min(SOA TTL, SOA MINIMUM)` (RFC 2308 §3, §5).
- **NXDOMAIN means nothing exists at or below the name** (RFC 8020 §2). So a server must never return NXDOMAIN for an empty non-terminal. `b.c.example.com` in the example zone exists only because `a.b.c.example.com` does, and it must get NODATA. Resolvers may cut off whole subtrees on NXDOMAIN, and a wrong NXDOMAIN for an ENT makes every name below it unreachable.

`nope.example.com A`, an NXDOMAIN (real server output; byte-identical to an independently generated test vector):

```text
0000  7f 01 84 03 00 01 00 00 00 01 00 00 04 6e 6f 70  .............nop
0010  65 07 65 78 61 6d 70 6c 65 03 63 6f 6d 00 00 01  e.example.com...
0020  00 01 c0 11 00 06 00 01 00 00 01 2c 00 27 03 6e  ...........,.'.n
0030  73 31 c0 11 0a 68 6f 73 74 6d 61 73 74 65 72 c0  s1...hostmaster.
0040  11 78 c3 dc f1 00 00 1c 20 00 00 0e 10 00 12 75  .x...... ......u
0050  00 00 00 01 2c                                   ....,
```

*85 octets.*

| Offset | Bytes | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 0x02 | `84 03` | QR=1, AA=1, RCODE **3 NXDOMAIN** |
| 0x08 | `00 01` | NSCOUNT 1 |
| 0x0c | `04 nope 07 example 03 com 00` | QNAME (`example.com` starts at 0x11) |
| 0x22 | `c0 11` | owner → `example.com.` (the zone apex, not QNAME) |
| 0x24 | `00 06 00 01` | SOA, IN |
| 0x28 | `00 00 01 2c` | TTL **300** = min(3600, MINIMUM 300) |
| 0x2c | `00 27` | RDLENGTH 39 |
| 0x2e | `03 6e 73 31 c0 11` | MNAME `ns1` + pointer → `ns1.example.com.` (6 octets) |
| 0x34 | `0a hostmaster c0 11` | RNAME `hostmaster.example.com.` (13 octets) |
| 0x41 | `78 c3 dc f1` … `00 00 01 2c` | SERIAL 2026102001, REFRESH 7200, RETRY 3600, EXPIRE 1209600, MINIMUM 300 (20 octets) |

`b.c.example.com TXT`, an ENT, gives NODATA. Note RCODE 0 in `84 00`:

```text
0000  7f 03 84 00 00 01 00 00 00 01 00 00 01 62 01 63  .............b.c
0010  07 65 78 61 6d 70 6c 65 03 63 6f 6d 00 00 10 00  .example.com....
0020  01 c0 10 00 06 00 01 00 00 01 2c 00 27 03 6e 73  ..........,.'.ns
0030  31 c0 10 0a 68 6f 73 74 6d 61 73 74 65 72 c0 10  1...hostmaster..
0040  78 c3 dc f1 00 00 1c 20 00 00 0e 10 00 12 75 00  x...... ......u.
0050  00 00 01 2c                                      ...,
```

*84 octets.*

### 11.7 Setting AA, and the RCODE in chains

- **AA=1** when the server is authoritative for the zone the answer comes from: positive answers, NODATA and NXDOMAIN from its zones.
- With a CNAME/DNAME chain in the answer, "the AA bit corresponds to the name which matches the query name, or the first owner name in the answer section" (RFC 1035 §4.1.1), and only that name (RFC 6604 §2.1).
- **AA=0 for referrals.** The NS records at a cut are the child's data, and the parent is not authoritative for them.
- **AA=0 for REFUSED, FORMERR, NOTIMP and SERVFAIL.**
- The **RCODE describes the last name in the chain** (RFC 6604 §3). `a CNAME b`, where `b` does not exist, gives **NXDOMAIN** with the CNAME in the answer section.

### 11.8 Referrals and the additional section

**Referral** (QNAME at or below a cut, step 3b):

| Section | Contents |
|---|---|
| Header | RCODE NOERROR, **AA=0** |
| Answer | empty |
| Authority | the NS RRset at the cut (plus DS and its RRSIG when DNSSEC is requested) |
| Additional | A/AAAA for the NS targets: **all in-domain glue is mandatory**, or set TC=1 if it does not fit (RFC 9471 §3.1). Sibling glue should be included when it fits (RFC 9471 §3.2). |

Real output for `host.sub.example.com A`, delegated to `ns.sub.example.com`:

```text
0000  00 42 80 00 00 01 00 00 00 01 00 01 04 68 6f 73  .B...........hos
0010  74 03 73 75 62 07 65 78 61 6d 70 6c 65 03 63 6f  t.sub.example.co
0020  6d 00 00 01 00 01 c0 11 00 02 00 01 00 00 0e 10  m...............
0030  00 05 02 6e 73 c0 11 c0 32 00 01 00 01 00 00 0e  ...ns...2.......
0040  10 00 04 c0 00 02 c8                             .......
```

*71 octets.*

| Offset | Bytes | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 0x02 | `80 00` | QR=1, **AA=0**, NOERROR |
| 0x04 | `00 01 00 00 00 01 00 01` | QD=1, AN=0, **NS=1, AR=1** |
| 0x26 | `c0 11 00 02 00 01 00 00 0e 10 00 05` | `sub.example.com.` NS, TTL 3600, RDLENGTH 5 |
| 0x32 | `02 6e 73 c0 11` | `ns` + pointer → `ns.sub.example.com.` |
| 0x37 | `c0 32 00 01 00 01 00 00 0e 10 00 04` | owner → 0x32 `ns.sub.example.com.`, A, TTL 3600, RDLENGTH 4 |
| 0x43 | `c0 00 02 c8` | glue 192.0.2.200 |

**DS queries at a cut** are the one exception to "names at a cut belong to the child". The DS RRset lives in the **parent** (RFC 4035 §3.1.4.1), so a query for `sub.example.com DS` is answered from the parent zone and not referred. A server that hosts both parent and child must look up DS in the parent (RFC 4035 §3.1.4.1). The §17 server always picks the most specific zone, so in that configuration it would answer DS from the child, which is wrong. Keep that in mind if you host both levels.

**Additional-section processing** (step 6) for positive answers: add the addresses of the targets of NS (RFC 1035 §3.3.11), MX (RFC 1035 §3.3.9) and SRV (RFC 2782) records, when you have them authoritatively. This is optional data. Drop it first when space runs out, without setting TC (RFC 2181 §9). The `example.com MX` answer from the §17 server, for instance, includes `mail.example.com A 192.0.2.25` in the additional section.

### 11.9 Minimal responses

Older servers also put the zone's NS RRset into the authority section of every positive answer, and the matching addresses into the additional section. That makes responses larger and helps amplification, and resolvers do not need it. Most current servers can be configured to leave it out (for example BIND's `minimal-responses` option). The §17 server never adds it.

## 12. Recursive Resolver Logic

### 12.1 What the resolver does

RFC 1034 §5.3.2–5.3.3 describes the resolver as a loop over four state variables. SNAME, STYPE and SCLASS are the name, type and class being sought. SLIST is the list of servers currently being tried, with statistics. SBELT is the "safety belt" list used when nothing better is known, i.e. the root hints. The loop has four steps:

1. Look in the cache. If the answer (or a negative answer) is there, return it.
2. Find the best servers to ask: the **closest enclosing zone whose NS records are cached**, or the root.
3. Send queries to those servers until one answers.
4. Analyse the response:
   - **a.** It answers the question or has a name error: cache it, return it.
   - **b.** It is a **referral** to a closer zone: cache the delegation and glue, go to step 2.
   - **c.** It is a **CNAME** that does not answer the question: cache it, change SNAME to the target, go to step 1.
   - **d.** It is a server failure or other bizarre content: remove the server from SLIST, go to step 3.

The §17 resolver follows that structure. `Resolver::resolve` does steps 1 and 4c across CNAME hops, and `Resolver::iterate` does steps 2, 3, 4a, 4b and 4d.

### 12.2 Root hints and priming

A resolver ships with the names and addresses of the root servers (`a.root-servers.net` … `m.root-servers.net`; the current list is published at `https://www.internic.net/domain/named.root`). At startup it sends a **priming query**: `. NS IN` to one of the hint addresses, with RD=0, a random source port, and EDNS announcing a payload size of at least 1024 (RFC 9609 §3). It then uses the *answer*, not the hints, and primes again only when that NS RRset expires (RFC 9609 §3.1). If a hint does not answer, it MUST retry with a different hint address (RFC 9609 §3.1). RFC 9609 obsoletes RFC 8109.

The §17 resolver uses the hint addresses directly whenever it has no closer delegation cached. That is simpler and still works, but it is not what RFC 9609 recommends.

### 12.3 Classifying a response

After sending an iterative query (RD=0) for `(SNAME, STYPE)` to a server for zone Z:

| Response | How to recognise it | Action |
|---|---|---|
| **Answer** | NOERROR, the answer has an RRset with owner SNAME and type STYPE (possibly after a CNAME chain inside the answer) | cache (rank "answer"), return |
| **CNAME** | answer has a CNAME for SNAME, no data for the target in this response | cache the CNAME, restart with SNAME = target |
| **Referral** | NOERROR, AA=0, empty answer, NS RRset in authority whose owner Z' is **below Z** and **at or above SNAME** | cache NS and acceptable glue, set Z = Z', go on to Z's servers |
| **NXDOMAIN** | RCODE 3; authority has the SOA of Z | negative-cache (name), return NXDOMAIN |
| **NODATA** | NOERROR, no answer for SNAME/STYPE, SOA in authority (or AA=1 with an empty answer) | negative-cache (name, type), return NOERROR / empty |
| **Lame / error** | SERVFAIL, REFUSED, NOTIMP, FORMERR; a referral that goes up or sideways (Z' not below Z); AA=0 with no referral; timeout | try the next server; if none is left, SERVFAIL |

A referral whose NS owner is **not strictly below** the zone you asked about is an "upward referral", typically sent by a misconfigured server. Following it can make you loop, so treat it as lame.

### 12.4 Bailiwick: what you may believe from whom

A server for zone Z can only tell you the truth about names **inside Z**. Everything else in its response is an unverified claim, and caching it uncritically allows **cache poisoning** (§16.1). The rules:

1. **Match the response to the query**: source address and port, destination address and port, ID, QNAME, QCLASS and QTYPE. On any mismatch, the response is invalid (RFC 5452 §9.1).
2. **Accept only in-domain records**: discard every record in every section whose owner is not equal to or below Z (RFC 5452 §6). The §17 resolver does this first:

   ```cpp
   // Bailiwick rule: a server for `zone` may only tell us about names inside `zone`.
   auto outside = [&](const ResourceRecord& rr) { return !rr.name.isSubdomainOf(zone); };
   std::erase_if(resp->answers, outside);
   std::erase_if(resp->authority, outside);
   std::erase_if(resp->additional, outside);
   ```

3. **Glue is accepted only for the referral being followed**, and only for names under Z. It is cached at low rank and never returned to clients as an answer (RFC 2181 §5.4.1).
4. **Rank data by trustworthiness** and never let lower-ranked data overwrite higher-ranked data (RFC 2181 §5.4.1). From most to least trustworthy:

| Rank | Source |
|---|---|
| 1 | Primary zone file data (not glue) |
| 2 | Zone transfer data (not glue) |
| 3 | Answer section of an authoritative (AA=1) reply |
| 4 | Authority section of an authoritative reply |
| 5 | Glue from a primary zone or zone transfer |
| 6 | Answer section of a non-authoritative reply |
| 7 | Additional section of an authoritative reply; authority section of a non-authoritative reply; additional section of a non-authoritative reply |

The §17 cache collapses this into two levels, `Trust::Answer` and `Trust::Glue`. Glue-rank data can be used to reach name servers, but never satisfies a client query. Note the caveat in RFC 2181 §5.4.1: in an authoritative answer that follows a CNAME, only the CNAME itself is necessarily authoritative. The §17 resolver accepts chain records in the same response only if they pass the bailiwick filter for the zone it asked.

### 12.5 QNAME minimisation (RFC 9156)

Sending the full `www.example.com` to the root and to `.com` tells them more than they need. With QNAME minimisation, the resolver reveals one more label at each step:

```text
to root:      com.               A?   → referral to com
to .com:      example.com.       A?   → referral to example.com
to example:   www.example.com.   A?   → answer
```

- Any QTYPE whose authority is below the cut works for the probing queries. **A or AAAA** are recommended, since middleboxes handle them best (RFC 9156 §2.1). The older advice was QTYPE NS.
- An **NXDOMAIN** for a minimised name means everything below it is gone too (RFC 8020), so the resolver can stop early. A **NOERROR/NODATA** means "no cut here", so it adds the next label.
- Resolvers **MUST** limit the number of outgoing queries per request (RFC 9156 §2.3). The recommended limit is MAX_MINIMISE_COUNT = 10 iterations, with only the first MINIMISE_ONE_LAB = 4 adding a single label each. Without it, a 100-label name under a wildcard costs 100 queries.
- Some broken servers answer NXDOMAIN for empty non-terminals. Production resolvers then fall back to the full QNAME.

The §17 resolver does **not** minimise; it sends the full QNAME, like the classic algorithm. To add minimisation, change `iterate()` to send `qname.suffix(zone.labelCount() + 1)` with type A until it reaches QNAME, treating NODATA as "continue" (RFC 9156 §3).

### 12.6 Choosing servers and handling failure

- **Server selection.** Production resolvers track a smoothed RTT per server address and prefer the fastest, while occasionally probing the others. The §17 resolver shuffles randomly.
- **Retries.** On timeout or a bad RCODE, try the next server (§8.6). Remember servers that failed, so you do not keep paying their timeout.
- **Name servers without glue.** If the NS names are outside the zone (e.g. `example.com NS ns.provider.net`), resolve their addresses first. That is a separate recursive lookup, so limit the depth (`kMaxDepth = 6` in §17). An NS name **inside** the zone with no glue cannot be resolved at all. The §17 resolver skips it instead of looping.
- **Limits.** Every loop needs a bound: referrals per lookup (24), CNAME hops (12), nested NS-address lookups (6), and a total time budget. The NXNSAttack (2020) used delegations with many glueless NS names to make resolvers send hundreds of queries per client query. Cap the number of NS names you resolve per delegation, too.
- **Failure caching.** Cache SERVFAIL outcomes for 1 s to 5 min (RFC 9520 §3.2). The §17 resolver does not implement this, which would be the first thing to add for production use.
- **DS queries** go to the **parent** zone's servers (RFC 4035 §3.1.4.1; RFC 9156 §3 step 1a). The §17 resolver starts its DS search one label above QNAME and never follows a referral into the child for DS.

### 12.7 Pseudocode

```text
resolve(qname, qtype, depth):
    if depth > MAX_DEPTH: return SERVFAIL
    answers := []
    repeat MAX_CNAME_HOPS times:
        if cache has NXDOMAIN for qname:           return NXDOMAIN, answers, soa
        if cache has (qname, qtype):               return NOERROR/NODATA, answers + cached
        if cache has (qname, CNAME):               answers += cname; qname := target; continue
        step := iterate(qname, qtype, depth)
        if step failed:                            return SERVFAIL
        answers += step.records
        if step is CNAME continuation:             qname := step.next; continue
        return step.rcode, answers, step.soa
    return SERVFAIL                                 ; chain too long

iterate(qname, qtype, depth):
    (zone, nsnames) := closest cached delegation for qname (parent side for DS), else root
    repeat MAX_REFERRALS times:
        servers := addresses(nsnames) or root hints
        resp := first valid NOERROR/NXDOMAIN reply from servers       ; §12.4 rule 1
        drop all records not under zone                               ; §12.4 rule 2
        follow CNAMEs for qname inside resp.answer, caching them
        if NXDOMAIN:                 cache negative; return NXDOMAIN
        if answer for (name, qtype): cache; return answer
        if CNAME chain without end:  return continue-at(target)
        if referral to child zone Z' (zone < Z' <= qname):
            cache NS (glue rank) and A glue under zone
            zone, nsnames := Z', NS targets; continue
        if SOA in authority:         cache NODATA; return NODATA
        return failure
```

The C++ is in §17.10.

### 12.8 Forwarding

A **forwarder** does not iterate. It sends the client's question, with RD=1, to one or more configured upstream recursive resolvers, then caches and relays the answer. This is the mode the `easydns` README describes ("Small DNS Forwarder"). The forwarding function, compiled and tested against a public resolver:

```cpp
// Forwarding: relay one question to an upstream recursive resolver with RD=1 and
// return its answer. The caller copies the answer into a response that carries the
// *client's* ID and question.
inline std::optional<Message> forward(const sockaddr_in& upstream, const Question& q) {
    Message out;
    out.header.id = random16();  // never reuse the client's ID upstream
    out.header.rd = true;
    out.questions.push_back(q);
    out.edns = Edns{};
    auto wire = serializeMessage(out);

    auto raw = exchangeUdp(upstream, wire, out.header.id, 2000);
    for (int attempt = 0; attempt < 2 && raw; ++attempt) {
        Message r;
        try {
            r = parseMessage(*raw);
        } catch (const ParseError&) {
            return std::nullopt;
        }
        if (!r.header.qr || r.questions.size() != 1 || !(r.questions[0].qname == q.qname) ||
            r.questions[0].qtype != q.qtype || r.questions[0].qclass != q.qclass)
            return std::nullopt;
        if (!r.header.tc) return r;
        raw = exchangeTcp(upstream, wire, 2000);  // truncated: same query over TCP
    }
    return std::nullopt;
}
```

Rules for forwarders:

- Use a **fresh random ID and source port** upstream (§16.2). Never pass the client's ID through. Map responses back to clients through your own pending-query table.
- Build the client's response yourself. Use the client's ID, its question exactly as received (case included), RA=1 and RD copied, the upstream RCODE and records, and **your own** OPT with *your* payload size, not the upstream's.
- Cache with the TTLs from upstream, and count them down (§13).
- Forward to resolvers you trust. A forwarder inherits all of its upstream's answers, including any poisoned ones.
- Never forward responses (QR=1), and apply the same access control as a resolver (§16.4).

## 13. Caching

### 13.1 What is cached

The unit of caching is the **RRset**, keyed by (owner name, type, class) with the name compared case-insensitively. Always cache and return whole RRsets, never single records. All records in an RRset have the same TTL (RFC 2181 §5.2). If a response has differing TTLs within one RRset, use the minimum.

Also cache **negative answers** (§13.3), and **resolution failures** briefly (RFC 9520 §3.2).

**Never** cache OPT or TSIG records (RFC 6891 §6.1.1), data from responses that failed validation (§12.4), records outside the bailiwick, or data from a TC=1 response (repeat the query over TCP instead).

### 13.2 TTL handling

- A record may be used for TTL seconds after it was received. Store an **absolute expiry time**, and on every cache hit return the **remaining** time as the TTL, so downstream caches do not extend the lifetime.
- TTL 0: use the record for the current transaction only, and do not store it (RFC 1035 §3.2.1). The §17 resolver returns the results of `iterate()` directly, so zero-TTL answers still reach the client.
- **Cap the TTL.** RFC 8767 §4 recommends at most 604,800 s (7 days). Many resolvers use 1 day by default; the §17 cache uses 86,400 s.
- Do **not** raise small TTLs to a minimum without good reason. Operators lower TTLs before planned changes, and a forced minimum breaks that.

The §17 cache returns copies with the TTL already counted down:

```cpp
// Returns a copy with TTLs reduced by the time spent in the cache.
std::optional<Entry> get(const DnsName& name, uint16_t type, Trust minTrust) {
    auto it = map_.find(key(name, type));
    if (it == map_.end()) return std::nullopt;
    auto now = Clock::now();
    if (now >= it->second.expires) {
        map_.erase(it);
        return std::nullopt;
    }
    if (it->second.trust < minTrust) return std::nullopt;
    Entry e = it->second;
    auto left = uint32_t(std::chrono::duration_cast<std::chrono::seconds>(e.expires - now).count());
    for (auto& rr : e.records) rr.ttl = left;
    if (e.soa) e.soa->ttl = left;
    return e;
}
```

Use a **monotonic clock** (`steady_clock`) for expiry. Wall-clock time jumps when NTP adjusts the clock. DNSSEC signature validity, by contrast, needs real wall-clock time (§15).

### 13.3 Negative caching (RFC 2308)

| Kind | Key | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| NXDOMAIN | (name, *any type*) | The name does not exist, so **no** type does, nor any name below it (RFC 8020) |
| NODATA | (name, type) | The name exists but not with this type |

- **TTL = min(SOA TTL, SOA MINIMUM)**, using the SOA from the authority section of the negative response (RFC 2308 §5). Cap it as well: RFC 2308 §5 says values of 1–3 hours "have been found to work well". The §17 cache uses 10,800 s.
- A negative response **without an SOA SHOULD NOT be cached** (RFC 2308 §5).
- When answering from a negative cache entry, include the SOA in the authority section with its TTL counted down (RFC 2308 §6).
- **RFC 8020:** after NXDOMAIN for `b.example.com`, a resolver may answer NXDOMAIN for `a.b.example.com` from the cache, without asking. The §17 cache does not do this; it keys NXDOMAIN by exact name only.
- **Aggressive use of NSEC/NSEC3** (RFC 8198): a validating resolver can use a cached, signed NSEC range to synthesise NXDOMAIN for *any* name in that range. That cuts off random-subdomain floods.

```cpp
// RFC 2308 §5: negative TTL = min(SOA TTL, SOA MINIMUM). NXDOMAIN covers every type.
void putNegative(const DnsName& name, uint16_t type, uint16_t rc, const ResourceRecord& soa) {
    const auto& rd = soa.rdata;
    uint32_t minimum = uint32_t(rd[rd.size() - 4]) << 24 | uint32_t(rd[rd.size() - 3]) << 16 |
                       uint32_t(rd[rd.size() - 2]) << 8 | rd[rd.size() - 1];
    uint32_t ttl = std::min({soa.ttl, minimum, kMaxNegativeTtl});
    Entry e;
    e.negative = true;
    e.rcode = rc;
    e.soa = soa;
    e.trust = Trust::Answer;
    e.stored = Clock::now();
    e.expires = e.stored + std::chrono::seconds(ttl);
    map_[key(name, rc == rcode::NXDOMAIN ? kNxdomainType : type)] = std::move(e);
}
```

### 13.4 Representing the cache

| Design point | Recommendation |
|---|---|
| Key | `lower-cased wire name ‖ type ‖ class`. The §17 code uses `name.key() + 2 type bytes`, because it handles IN only. |
| Value | records (uncompressed RDATA), expiry, trust rank, negative flag + SOA, DNSSEC validation status |
| Lookup order | NXDOMAIN entry for the name → exact (name, type) → (name, CNAME) when type ≠ CNAME → closest cached NS for the delegation search |
| Eviction | Bound the memory. Use LRU, or random eviction among expired entries first. Attackers can fill caches with random names. |
| Concurrency | Shard the map by hash, with one lock per shard |
| Separate entries | Answers to DO=1 queries carry RRSIGs, and CD=1 answers may include unvalidated data. Keep validation state per RRset. ECS answers are keyed by client subnet as well (RFC 7871 §7.3). |

### 13.5 Replacement rules

New data replaces cached data only if it is **at least as trustworthy** (RFC 2181 §5.4.1; table in §12.4). In particular, glue or additional-section data must never replace an authoritative answer. The §17 `putRRset` enforces this:

```cpp
if (auto it = map_.find(k); it != map_.end() && it->second.trust > trust && Clock::now() < it->second.expires)
    return;   // keep the more trustworthy, unexpired entry
```

A related rule: do not let a cached NS RRset be extended indefinitely by referrals from the parent. "Ghost domain" attacks kept deleted domains alive in caches that way. Modern resolvers refresh delegations from the parent and limit NS TTLs to the parent's TTL. See CVE-2012-1033 and the paper "Ghost Domain Names" (NDSS 2012).

### 13.6 Serving stale data (RFC 8767)

When the authoritative servers are unreachable, a resolver **may** answer with expired records instead of SERVFAIL:

- Give stale records a **TTL > 0, recommended 30 s** (RFC 8767 §4), so clients come back soon.
- Keep stale data only for a bounded time. The suggested maximum stale timer is 1–3 days (RFC 8767 §5).
- Only an authoritative NOERROR or NXDOMAIN counts as a refresh. Other RCODEs leave the stale data in place (RFC 8767 §4).
- Mark such answers with Extended DNS Error 3 (Stale Answer), or 19 for a stale NXDOMAIN (§9.5.4).

### 13.7 Prefetching

Popular records can be refreshed **before** they expire, for example when a query arrives within the last 10% of the TTL. Clients then never wait on a cache miss. Unbound's `prefetch` option works this way. It is a performance feature, not a protocol requirement.

## 14. Zone Transfers, NOTIFY, UPDATE and TSIG

These are the protocols between a zone's servers: a primary that holds the master copy, and secondaries that replicate it. The §17 server answers them with REFUSED or NOTIMP. This section gives what you need to implement them.

### 14.1 AXFR: full zone transfer (RFC 5936)

**Query.** An ordinary QUERY with QTYPE = AXFR (252), sent **over TCP**. AXFR over UDP is not defined (RFC 5936 §4.2).

```text
0000  77 03 00 00 00 01 00 00 00 00 00 00 07 65 78 61  w............exa
0010  6d 70 6c 65 03 63 6f 6d 00 00 fc 00 01           mple.com.....
```

*29 octets.*

`77 03` ID · `00 00` flags (all clear) · QD=1 · `07 example 03 com 00` · `00 fc` = 252 AXFR · `00 01` IN.

**Response.** A *series* of messages on the same TCP connection, all with the query's ID (RFC 5936 §2.2):

```text
message 1:  header(AA=1, ANCOUNT=n1) question  SOA  rr  rr  rr …
message 2:  header(AA=1, ANCOUNT=n2) [question] rr  rr  rr …
   …
message k:  header(AA=1, ANCOUNT=nk) [question] rr … SOA      ← the same SOA again ends the transfer
```

- The first message **must begin with** the zone's SOA, and the last **must end with** the same SOA. No other message contains the SOA (RFC 5936 §2.2).
- The question is copied into the first message, and may be repeated or left empty in later ones (RFC 5936 §2.2).
- The other RRs come in **any order and grouping**; clients must accept any order and ignore duplicates (RFC 5936 §2.2). Include glue and delegations (RFC 5936 §3.2, §3.3), but not occluded data (RFC 5936 §3.5).
- **Preserve case** in owner names and RDATA (RFC 5936 §3.4). Compression is allowed as usual, within one message.
- Pack each message with as many RRs as fit (up to 65,535 bytes).
- **Authorise transfers** with an IP ACL and/or TSIG (§14.5). Answer unauthorised requests with REFUSED (RFC 5936 §5). An open AXFR hands out your whole zone and is a popular reconnaissance target.

A **secondary** on refresh: query the primary for the SOA. If `serialLess(local, remote)` (§10.5), transfer with IXFR or AXFR, build the new zone in memory, and swap it in **atomically** only after the final SOA has arrived.

### 14.2 IXFR: incremental transfer (RFC 1995)

**Query.** QTYPE = IXFR (251), with the client's current **SOA in the authority section**, which tells the server which version the client has (RFC 1995 §3). UDP may be tried first.

**Response** (RFC 1995 §2, §4):

| Case | Response |
|---|---|
| Client is up to date (its serial ≥ server's) | a single SOA (the server's current one) |
| Server has no history for that serial | a full AXFR-style answer (SOA … SOA), with QTYPE IXFR |
| Incremental | `SOA(new)`, then for each version step from oldest to newest: `SOA(old) <deleted RRs> SOA(next) <added RRs>`, then `SOA(new)` again |
| Over UDP and too large | a single SOA, which tells the client to retry over TCP |

Example: the client has serial 1, the server has 3. The response is

```text
SOA 3                      ; current version
SOA 1   -a  -b             ; delete from version 1 ...
SOA 2   +c                 ; ... add to reach version 2
SOA 2   -c                 ; delete from version 2 ...
SOA 3   +d  +e             ; ... add to reach version 3
SOA 3                      ; end
```

The structure is told apart from AXFR by the **second RR being an SOA** (RFC 1995 §4). A client applies all differences before it switches to the new version.

### 14.3 NOTIFY (RFC 1996)

So secondaries don't have to wait for REFRESH, the primary sends **NOTIFY** (opcode 4) to each secondary when the zone changes:

```text
0000  66 02 24 00 00 01 00 00 00 00 00 00 07 65 78 61  f.$..........exa
0010  6d 70 6c 65 03 63 6f 6d 00 00 06 00 01           mple.com.....
```

*29 octets.*

`66 02` ID · `24 00` = `0010 0100 0000 0000`: QR=0, **opcode 4**, **AA=1** · QD=1 · question `example.com SOA IN`.

- The question names the zone, with QTYPE SOA. The answer section may carry the new SOA as an *unsecured hint* (RFC 1996 §3.7).
- The secondary replies with the same message with QR=1 (RFC 1996 §3.3). It then behaves as if REFRESH had expired: it queries the primary's SOA and transfers if the serial grew (RFC 1996 §3.11).
- NOTIFY is usually sent over UDP. The primary retransmits periodically until it gets a response (RFC 1996 §3.6).
- A secondary should ignore NOTIFY from hosts that are not its configured primaries (RFC 1996 §3.10), or require TSIG.

### 14.4 Dynamic UPDATE (RFC 2136)

Opcode 5. The four sections are renamed: **Zone** (count ZOCOUNT; one entry with the zone name, type SOA and class), **Prerequisite** (PRCOUNT), **Update** (UPCOUNT) and **Additional** (ADCOUNT) (RFC 2136 §2.2–2.6). Every record uses the normal RR format. The meaning comes from the CLASS / TYPE / TTL / RDATA combination:

**Prerequisites** (RFC 2136 §2.4). All must hold, or the update is rejected with the RCODE shown:

| CLASS | TYPE | RDATA | Meaning | Failure RCODE |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ANY | ANY | empty | Name is in use | NXDOMAIN |
| NONE | ANY | empty | Name is not in use | YXDOMAIN |
| ANY | T | empty | RRset of type T exists (value-independent) | NXRRSET |
| NONE | T | empty | RRset of type T does not exist | YXRRSET |
| zone class | T | data | RRset exists with exactly these RRs (value-dependent) | NXRRSET |

**Updates** (RFC 2136 §2.5):

| CLASS | TYPE | TTL | RDATA | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| zone class | T | ttl | data | **Add** the RR to the RRset |
| ANY | T | 0 | empty | **Delete the RRset** of type T at the name |
| ANY | ANY | 0 | empty | **Delete all RRsets** at the name |
| NONE | T | 0 | data | **Delete one RR** that matches the data |

Adding `new.example.com 300 A 192.0.2.77`:

```text
0000  55 01 28 00 00 01 00 00 00 01 00 00 07 65 78 61  U.(..........exa
0010  6d 70 6c 65 03 63 6f 6d 00 00 06 00 01 03 6e 65  mple.com......ne
0020  77 c0 0c 00 01 00 01 00 00 01 2c 00 04 c0 00 02  w.........,.....
0030  4d                                               M
```

*49 octets.*

| Offset | Bytes | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 0x02 | `28 00` | `0010 1000 …`: QR=0, **opcode 5 (UPDATE)** |
| 0x04 | `00 01 00 00 00 01 00 00` | ZOCOUNT 1, PRCOUNT 0, UPCOUNT 1, ADCOUNT 0 |
| 0x0c | `07 example 03 com 00 00 06 00 01` | Zone section: `example.com.` SOA IN |
| 0x1d | `03 6e 65 77 c0 0c` | Update RR owner `new` + pointer → `new.example.com.` |
| 0x23 | `00 01 00 01 00 00 01 2c 00 04 c0 00 02 4d` | A, class IN (= add), TTL 300, RDLENGTH 4, 192.0.2.77 |

Server processing order (RFC 2136 §3): check the zone section (NOTAUTH if not authoritative; NOTZONE for names outside the zone), then authorisation (REFUSED), then prerequisites (atomically), then apply the updates and **increment the SOA serial**, and write the change to stable storage before replying. Updates are **all or nothing**. In practice they must be authenticated with TSIG (RFC 3007 for secure dynamic update).

### 14.5 TSIG: transaction signatures (RFC 8945)

TSIG authenticates a single message (or a TCP stream of them) with a **shared secret** and an HMAC. It protects zone transfers, NOTIFY and UPDATE between servers that trust each other. It is a pseudo-RR, and it **must be the last record in the additional section**:

| RR field | Value |
|---|---|
| NAME | key name, e.g. `xfr-key.example.com.` |
| TYPE | 250 (TSIG) |
| CLASS | ANY (255) |
| TTL | 0 |

RDATA (RFC 8945 §4.2):

| Field | Size | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Algorithm Name | name | e.g. `hmac-sha256.`, which **MUST** be implemented (RFC 8945 §6) |
| Time Signed | 48 bits | seconds since 1970 UTC |
| Fudge | 16 bits | permitted clock skew, in seconds; RECOMMENDED 300 (RFC 8945 §10) |
| MAC Size + MAC | 16 + n | the HMAC |
| Original ID | 16 bits | the message ID before any forwarder changed it |
| Error | 16 bits | extended RCODE, e.g. BADSIG 16, BADKEY 17, BADTIME 18 |
| Other Len + Other Data | 16 + n | on BADTIME, the server's time |

**What is MACed** (RFC 8945 §4.3):

1. For a **response**: the request's MAC (2-byte MAC Size + MAC) first (RFC 8945 §4.3.1).
2. The DNS message **without** the TSIG RR, with ARCOUNT decremented and the header ID set to Original ID (RFC 8945 §4.3.2).
3. The "TSIG variables" (RFC 8945 §4.3.3): key NAME and Algorithm Name in canonical (lower-case, uncompressed) wire form, CLASS, TTL, Time Signed, Fudge, Error, Other Len, Other Data. RDLENGTH, MAC Size, MAC and Original ID are excluded.

**Verification:** look up the key by name and algorithm (BADKEY if unknown), recompute and compare the MAC **in constant time** (BADSIG on mismatch), then check `|now − Time Signed| ≤ Fudge` (BADTIME). On BADTIME, report the server's time in Other Data. RFC 8945 §5.2 specifies the order. CVE-2017-3143 (cited in RFC 8945) was a real TSIG validation bypass in BIND, so test these paths carefully.

For long TCP transfers, every message in the stream is signed, and each MAC chains to the previous one (RFC 8945 §5.3.1).

## 15. DNSSEC

### 15.1 What DNSSEC does and does not do

DNSSEC (RFC 4033, 4034, 4035) adds **digital signatures** to DNS data. A validating resolver can then check that:

- an RRset really comes from the zone's owner and was not modified (**data origin authentication and integrity**);
- a name or type really does **not** exist (**authenticated denial of existence**).

It does **not** encrypt anything, so there is no confidentiality (for that, see DoT/DoH/DoQ in §8.7). It does not protect the last hop between a stub and its resolver unless the stub validates itself. It does not authenticate the *query* or the server. Signatures are made offline or by the zone's signer, and anyone can serve them; caches and secondaries need no keys.

### 15.2 The pieces

| Element | Where | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| **DNSKEY** (§6.16) | zone apex | the zone's public keys |
| **RRSIG** (§6.17) | next to every signed RRset | signature over one RRset |
| **DS** (§6.18) | **parent** zone, at the cut | hash of a child DNSKEY; the link in the chain |
| **NSEC / NSEC3** (§6.19) | every name / hashed name | signed "nothing exists between X and Y" |
| **DO** bit (OPT) | query | "send me DNSSEC records" (RFC 3225) |
| **AD** bit (header) | response | "I validated this" (RFC 4035 §3.2.3) |
| **CD** bit (header) | query | "don't validate for me, I'll do it" (RFC 4035 §3.2.2) |

**Key roles.** Zones usually have a **KSK** (key signing key, flags 257, SEP bit set) that signs only the DNSKEY RRset and is referenced by the parent's DS. They also have a **ZSK** (zone signing key, flags 256) that signs everything else. This lets the ZSK be rolled without involving the parent. A single **CSK** (combined key) is also fine. Validators do not care about the roles. They only check the Zone Key bit (RFC 4035 §5.3.1).

### 15.3 The chain of trust

```text
 trust anchor (configured):  . DS/DNSKEY  key tag 20326 / 38696 (root KSKs)
        │ validates
        ▼
 .            DNSKEY RRset  ── signed by root KSK
        │ root ZSK signs:
 com.         DS            ── hash of com's KSK
        ▼
 com.         DNSKEY RRset  ── must contain a key matching that DS; signed by it
        │ com ZSK signs:
 example.com. DS
        ▼
 example.com. DNSKEY RRset  ── matches DS, self-signed by its KSK
        │ example.com ZSK signs:
 www.example.com. A + RRSIG ✔ Secure
```

- The **trust anchor** is configured in the validator. For the root, IANA publishes it at `https://data.iana.org/root-anchors/root-anchors.xml`. As of October 2026 that file lists **KSK-2017 (key tag 20326)** and **KSK-2024 (key tag 38696)**, both algorithm 8 (RSA/SHA-256). Validators should trust what the file lists and follow key rollovers automatically, as RFC 5011 describes.
- At every cut, the parent's signed **DS** says which child key to trust. If the parent proves that **no DS exists** (with signed NSEC/NSEC3), the child zone is **insecure**: unsigned, and acceptable. If a DS exists but the child's signatures do not validate, the data is **bogus**.

### 15.4 Key tags and DS digests

The **key tag** is a 16-bit checksum of the DNSKEY RDATA. It is used to find the right key quickly (RFC 4034 Appendix B). It is not unique, so always try every key that matches.

```cpp
// RFC 4034 Appendix B key tag over the full DNSKEY RDATA (not for algorithm 1).
inline uint16_t keyTag(std::span<const uint8_t> rdata) {
    uint32_t ac = 0;
    for (size_t i = 0; i < rdata.size(); ++i) ac += (i & 1) ? rdata[i] : uint32_t(rdata[i]) << 8;
    ac += (ac >> 16) & 0xFFFF;
    return uint16_t(ac & 0xFFFF);
}
```

Tested on the real root KSK-2017 DNSKEY RDATA (264 octets: `01 01 03 08` + the RSA key): the result is **20326**, which matches IANA's trust anchor file. The placeholder DNSKEY in §6.16 has key tag 61465. The DS example in §6.18 uses an unrelated tag of 12345, chosen only to show the layout.

The **DS digest** is `digest(owner name in canonical wire form ‖ DNSKEY RDATA)` (RFC 4034 §5.1.4). For the root, the owner is the single octet `00`. `SHA-256(00 ‖ RDATA of KSK-2017)` = `E06D44B80B8F1D39A95C0B0D7C65D08458E880409BBC683457104237C7F8EC8D`, exactly the digest in the IANA file. Use a crypto library (OpenSSL, libsodium, …) for the hash. Writing your own crypto is out of scope here.

### 15.5 Canonical form and ordering

Signatures are computed over a **canonical** byte representation, so that every validator reconstructs exactly the signed bytes regardless of compression or case.

**Canonical name order** (RFC 4034 §6.1). Compare names label by label **from the right**, each label as a lower-cased unsigned octet string, where a shorter string sorts first, and a name sorts before its descendants. This order defines the NSEC chain.

```cpp
// RFC 4034 §6.1 canonical name order: compare labels right to left, each as a lower-cased
// unsigned octet string, where a shorter string sorts first. Returns <0, 0, >0.
inline int canonicalCompare(const DnsName& a, const DnsName& b) {
    const auto& la = a.labels();
    const auto& lb = b.labels();
    size_t n = std::min(la.size(), lb.size());
    for (size_t i = 1; i <= n; ++i) {
        const std::string& x = la[la.size() - i];
        const std::string& y = lb[lb.size() - i];
        size_t m = std::min(x.size(), y.size());
        for (size_t k = 0; k < m; ++k) {
            auto cx = uint8_t(asciiLower(x[k])), cy = uint8_t(asciiLower(y[k]));
            if (cx != cy) return cx < cy ? -1 : 1;
        }
        if (x.size() != y.size()) return x.size() < y.size() ? -1 : 1;
    }
    if (la.size() != lb.size()) return la.size() < lb.size() ? -1 : 1;  // the ancestor sorts first
    return 0;
}
```

Sorting a shuffled copy of the RFC 4034 §6.1 example with this function reproduces the RFC's order: `example`, `a.example`, `yljkjljk.a.example`, `Z.a.example`, `zABC.a.EXAMPLE`, `z.example`, `\001.z.example`, `*.z.example`, `\200.z.example`. Note that `*` (0x2A) sorts between `\001` and `\200`.

**Canonical RR form** (RFC 4034 §6.2, as corrected by RFC 6840 §5.1):

1. No name compression anywhere.
2. Owner name in lower case.
3. Names **inside RDATA** in lower case for the types NS, MD, MF, CNAME, SOA, MB, MG, MR, PTR, MINFO, MX, RP, AFSDB, RT, SIG, PX, NXT, NAPTR, KX, SRV, DNAME, A6 and RRSIG. **Not** for NSEC (RFC 6840 §5.1). HINFO contains no names (RFC 6840 §5.1).
4. A wildcard-expanded RR uses the original `*.` owner (see Labels below).
5. TTL = the **Original TTL** from the RRSIG.

**Canonical RR order within an RRset** (RFC 4034 §6.3): sort by the canonical RDATA bytes. Duplicates are a protocol error and are removed.

**What is signed** (RFC 4034 §3.1.8.1):

```text
signature = sign( RRSIG_RDATA_without_signature  |  RR(1) | RR(2) | … )
RR(i)     = owner | type | class | original TTL | RDLENGTH | RDATA     (canonical form, sorted)
owner     = if RRSIG.Labels < labels(owner): "*." + the rightmost Labels labels of the owner
```

The RRSIG's own Signer's Name is also uncompressed and lower case.

### 15.6 Authenticated denial of existence

#### NSEC (RFC 4034 §4, RFC 4035 §3.1.3, §5.4)

The zone's names, sorted canonically, form a chain. Each name has an NSEC record pointing to the next one, and the last points back to the apex. `a.example.com NSEC d.example.com A RRSIG NSEC` therefore proves that `b.example.com` and `c.example.com` do not exist, and that `a.example.com` has only A, RRSIG and NSEC.

| Response | NSEC records required |
|---|---|
| **NODATA** | An NSEC **matching QNAME** whose bitmap lacks QTYPE **and** CNAME (RFC 4035 §3.1.3.1) |
| **NXDOMAIN** | An NSEC **covering QNAME** (QNAME falls between owner and next), **and** an NSEC proving no wildcard `*.<closest encloser>` exists (RFC 4035 §3.1.3.2) |
| **Wildcard answer** | The RRSIG's Labels field is less than the owner's label count, plus an NSEC covering QNAME, proving the exact name does not exist (RFC 4035 §3.1.3.3) |
| **Wildcard NODATA** | NSEC covering QNAME + NSEC matching the wildcard without the type (RFC 4035 §3.1.3.4) |
| **Insecure referral** | NSEC at the cut, with NS set and **no DS** bit (RFC 4035 §3.1.4) |

The weakness of NSEC is that anyone can walk the chain and list the whole zone ("zone walking"). The alternatives are NSEC3, or "minimally covering" NSEC records synthesised on the fly by online signers (RFC 4470).

#### NSEC3 (RFC 5155)

NSEC3 chains **hashes** of the owner names instead:

```text
H(name) = IH(salt, canonical-wire(name), iterations)
IH(salt, x, 0) = SHA1(x ‖ salt)
IH(salt, x, k) = SHA1(IH(salt, x, k−1) ‖ salt)        ; RFC 5155 §5
owner of the NSEC3 RR = base32hex(H(name)) . zone
```

Verified test vectors: RFC 5155 Appendix A uses salt `aabbccdd` and 12 iterations, giving `H(example) = 0p9mhaveqvm6t7vbl5lop2u3t2rp3tom` and `H(a.example) = 35mthgpgcu1qg68fab165klnsnk3dpvl`. With today's recommended parameters, 0 iterations and an empty salt (RFC 9276 §3.1), `H(example.com) = onib9mgub9h0rml3cdf5bgrj59dkjhvk`. Base32hex is the RFC 4648 §7 alphabet `0–9a–v`.

Because hashes have no hierarchy, proofs use the **closest encloser proof** (RFC 5155 §7.2.1):

1. An NSEC3 that **matches** the closest encloser, proving it exists.
2. An NSEC3 that **covers** the *next closer name* (closest encloser + one more QNAME label), proving the chain stops there.

| Response | NSEC3 records |
|---|---|
| NXDOMAIN | closest encloser proof + NSEC3 **covering** `*.<closest encloser>` (RFC 5155 §7.2.2) |
| NODATA (QTYPE ≠ DS) | NSEC3 **matching** QNAME, bitmap without QTYPE and CNAME (RFC 5155 §7.2.3) |
| NODATA (QTYPE = DS) | matching NSEC3; or, with opt-out, closest encloser proof with the Opt-Out flag on the covering NSEC3 (RFC 5155 §7.2.4) |
| Wildcard answer | NSEC3 covering the next closer name (RFC 5155 §7.2.6) |
| Referral to an unsigned child | matching NSEC3 without DS, or opt-out covering proof (RFC 5155 §7.2.7) |

**Opt-out** (flag bit 0x01, RFC 5155 §6): the NSEC3 chain may skip unsigned delegations. Large TLDs use it so they do not need NSEC3 records for millions of unsigned children. The trade-off is that unsigned delegations in that range can be added without detection.

Validators **MAY** treat NSEC3 records with iterations > 0 as insecure, or answer SERVFAIL (RFC 9276 §3.2). When they do, after validating the NSEC3 signature, they SHOULD attach EDE 27, "Unsupported NSEC3 Iterations Value".

### 15.7 The DO, AD and CD bits

| Bit | Who sets it | Rule |
|---|---|---|
| **DO** (in OPT) | requester | Without DO=1, servers **omit** RRSIG/NSEC/NSEC3 except when explicitly asked for (RFC 4035 §3.2.1). A server that supports DNSSEC copies DO into its response (RFC 3225; RFC 8906 §3.2.6). |
| **CD** | requester | "Checking Disabled": a validating resolver returns data even if it failed validation, so the client can validate itself. A recursive server **MUST copy CD** to the response (RFC 4035 §3.2.2). Authoritative servers SHOULD clear it (RFC 4035 §3.1.6). |
| **AD** in a response | validating resolver | Set only if **all** RRsets in the answer and authority sections are validated as Secure (RFC 4035 §3.2.3), and only if the query had DO=1 or AD=1 (RFC 6840 §5.8). Authoritative servers normally do not set it (RFC 4035 §3.1.6). |
| **AD** in a query | stub | "I understand AD; tell me" (RFC 6840 §5.7) |

AD is only as trustworthy as the path to the resolver. A stub should rely on it only over a secured channel, such as DoT, or to a resolver on localhost.

### 15.8 What an authoritative server must do when DO=1

1. Include the RRSIGs for every RRset in the answer, authority and additional sections, in the same section (RFC 4035 §3.1.1).
2. If RRSIGs for answer or authority RRsets don't fit, set **TC=1**. Dropping additional-section RRSIGs is allowed and **does not** require TC (RFC 4035 §3.1.1).
3. For negative answers, include the SOA, its RRSIG and the NSEC/NSEC3 proofs (RFC 4035 §3.1.3; RFC 5155 §7.2).
4. In referrals, include the DS RRset and its RRSIG, or an NSEC/NSEC3 proof that no DS exists (RFC 4035 §3.1.4).
5. Answer DS queries from the parent side (§11.8; RFC 4035 §3.1.4.1).
6. Do not validate your own authoritative data (RFC 4035 §3.1.6).

### 15.9 Validation algorithm

Security status of each RRset (RFC 4035 §4.3): **Secure** (a chain from a trust anchor validates), **Insecure** (provably no chain, e.g. an unsigned delegation), **Bogus** (a chain should exist but fails), or **Indeterminate** (no way to tell). Bogus answers become **SERVFAIL**, unless the client set CD (RFC 4035 §5.5), with EDE 6 "DNSSEC Bogus" or a more specific code.

```text
validate(rrset, rrsigs, zone Z):
    keys := trusted_dnskeys(Z)                     ; recursive, see below
    if keys == INSECURE: return Insecure
    for each sig in rrsigs:                        ; RFC 4035 §5.3.1
        require sig.owner == rrset.owner and sig.class == rrset.class
        require sig.signer == Z and rrset.owner is at or below Z
        require sig.type_covered == rrset.type
        require sig.labels <= labelcount(rrset.owner)          ; (excluding a leading "*")
        require inception <= now <= expiration                 ; serial arithmetic, RFC 4034 §3.1.5
        for each key in keys with key.tag == sig.keytag and key.alg == sig.alg
                                 and key.flags has ZONE (256) and key.protocol == 3:
            data := canonical(sig RDATA without signature) ‖ canonical sorted RRs   ; §15.5
            if verify(key, sig.signature, data): 
                if sig.labels < labelcount(owner): require proof that QNAME itself does not exist
                ttl := min(rrset TTL, sig TTL, sig.original_ttl, expiration − now)   ; RFC 4035 §5.3.3
                return Secure
    return Bogus

trusted_dnskeys(Z):
    if Z is a trust anchor: dnskeys := DNSKEY RRset of Z, must contain a key matching the anchor,
                            and the RRset must be self-signed by that key
    else:
        parent := zone above Z
        pk := trusted_dnskeys(parent)
        if pk == INSECURE: return INSECURE
        ds := DS RRset for Z from parent, validate(ds, …, parent)
        if parent proves (NSEC/NSEC3, validated) that no DS exists: return INSECURE
        if ds not Secure: return BOGUS
        dnskeys := DNSKEY RRset of Z
        require some key k in dnskeys with digest(Z ‖ k) == some ds entry using a supported
                digest type and algorithm, and dnskeys is signed by k              ; RFC 4035 §5.2
        if no DS entry uses a supported algorithm/digest: return INSECURE         ; RFC 4035 §5.2, RFC 6840 §5.2
    return dnskeys
```

Practical points:

- Cache validated DNSKEY and DS sets. Validation is CPU-bound, and a cold chain needs several queries per zone.
- Use the **current wall-clock time** for inception and expiration checks. A validator with a wrong clock fails everything.
- If no DS uses an algorithm or digest you support, treat the zone as **unsigned (Insecure)**, not Bogus (RFC 4035 §5.2; RFC 6840 §5.2).
- A wildcard-expanded positive answer must come with proof that the exact name does not exist (RFC 4035 §5.3.4).
- Synthesised CNAMEs from DNAME are unsigned. Validate the DNAME instead, and check that the CNAME follows from it (RFC 4035 §4.8).

### 15.10 Algorithms

The implementation and usage recommendations used to live in RFC 8624. Since RFC 9904 (November 2025) they are maintained **in the IANA registry itself** (summarised from the registry, October 2026):

| No. | Mnemonic | Use for signing | Use for validation | Implement for validation |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 5 | RSASHA1 | MUST NOT | RECOMMENDED | MUST |
| 7 | RSASHA1-NSEC3-SHA1 | MUST NOT | RECOMMENDED | MUST |
| 8 | RSASHA256 | RECOMMENDED | RECOMMENDED | MUST |
| 10 | RSASHA512 | NOT RECOMMENDED | RECOMMENDED | MUST |
| 13 | ECDSAP256SHA256 | RECOMMENDED | RECOMMENDED | MUST |
| 14 | ECDSAP384SHA384 | MAY | RECOMMENDED | RECOMMENDED |
| 15 | ED25519 | RECOMMENDED | RECOMMENDED | RECOMMENDED |
| 16 | ED448 | MAY | RECOMMENDED | RECOMMENDED |
| 1, 3, 6, 12 | RSAMD5, DSA, DSA-NSEC3-SHA1, ECC-GOST | MUST NOT | MUST NOT | MUST NOT |

For a new signer, use **13** (small keys and signatures) or **15**. The full list, including newer entries such as 17 (SM2SM3), 18 (ML-DSA-44) and 23 (ECC-GOST12), is in §19.5.

### 15.11 Out of scope

The following are not covered in depth here, and you should consult the references. The mathematics and the safe implementation of RSA, ECDSA and EdDSA: use a vetted library.
